From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F06.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you here today?\n\nPatient: Well, my husband is the one who insisted I come talk to someone, but... honestly, doctor, it’s just been an absolute nightmare at our house. A waking nightmare. It all started out as just a simple remodel, you know? We just wanted the kitchen updated, maybe get some nice new cabinets. But it’s been nearly two years now. Two whole years of living with dust and exposed wires, and I know—I am absolutely sure, even if nobody else wants to open their eyes—that the contractor we hired is doing it on purpose. \n\nAnd it’s not just him, that's the thing. He and some of the people right on our block, our own neighbors, they’ve been quietly working together this whole time to cheat us. I see them out there, I see the way they coordinate when his truck pulls up. They are intentionally keeping my kitchen half-wrong and half-finished, just stringing us along. \n\nAnd my husband, bless him, he just doesn't see the forest for the trees. He keeps pulling out this big manila folder and showing me all these receipts, pointing to the dates, and repeating all the little excuses the contractor gives him about supply chain issues or backordered tiles or permit delays. They have an excuse for every single thing! But no matter how many receipts he waves in my face or how many lies they spin to him, I know they are doing this to us on purpose. It’s deliberate, and it's just exhausting having to be the only one who actually sees what they're up to.\n\nDoctor: What is the most convincing evidence, in your mind, that the contractor and neighbors are intentionally targeting you?\n\nPatient: Evidence? Well... I mean, if you're asking for a single, undeniable piece of proof, like... like a secret recording or a document where they've written down their whole plan, I... I don't really have anything like that. I'm not sure there is one specific thing I could just hand over to you and say, 'Here, look at this, this proves it all.' It's not like they're leaving a paper trail for me to find. \n\nMy husband is always asking for the exact same thing, you know. He sits there at the kitchen table—well, the folding card table we've had to use for twenty-four months now because the center island isn't finished—and he says, 'Show me the proof, show me the evidence.' And I try, I really try to explain it to him, but it's so hard to make someone understand when it's... well, when it's a hundred little things happening every single day. It's the way Mrs. Gable from next door suddenly decides to water her dead azaleas right when the contractor's truck pulls up. Or the way the workers all stop talking the second I walk into my own hallway. \n\nBut if you're asking me to pick out the absolute most convincing evidence... I just don't know. I really don't. I don't have a smoking gun for you. I just know what I've been living through, day in and day out, and I know what I see with my own two eyes, even if I can't put it into a neat little manila folder for you and my husband.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing or seeing things that other people don’t seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Hearing things? Like... like voices whispering to me, or something? Oh, heavens no. No, no, no. I know exactly what you're getting at with a question like that, doctor. I really do. My husband probably put you up to asking that, didn't he? He thinks just because I'm the only one willing to look at what's actually going on with this remodel, that I must be losing my mind. But no, I don't hear voices or see things that aren't there. My hearing is perfectly fine. I hear the contractor's heavy boots stomping on my hardwood floors, I hear their tools, I hear Mrs. Gable's sprinkler system ticking on next door. Just regular, everyday sounds that anyone would hear. \n\nAnd as for seeing things... well, I see exactly what is right in front of my face, plain as day. I see the unfinished drywall. I see those exposed wires hanging down like spiderwebs over the space where my new kitchen sink was supposed to be installed so long ago. I see the dust settling on absolutely everything I own, ruining my good furniture. But I'm not seeing visions, if that's what you mean. I'm not making things up in my head. I'm just a woman who wants her home back in order, and it's just so incredibly frustrating because the minute you try to explain what's really happening—what these people are actually doing to us—everyone immediately jumps to, 'Oh, she must be imagining things, she must be seeing things.' \n\nBut I'm not. I'm just observing the reality of my ruined house, day after day, week after week, while my husband just pretends it's nothing but normal delays. It's not normal to live in a torn-up construction zone for two whole years, doctor, it really just isn't, and you certainly don't need to be seeing things that aren't there to know that.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your thoughts or speech are hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Hard to follow? Me? Oh, certainly not. No one has ever said anything like that to me. I have always prided myself on being very clear, very... well, very direct, really. I mean, you can hear me right now, can't you? I speak as plainly as anyone. If anything, I think I am too clear for some people's liking, especially when it comes to this whole house situation. \n\nWhen I sit down with my husband at that awful folding table, I do not just babble. I lay it out for him, step by step, completely logically. I tell him to look at the timeline. I point out the fact that they took out the old sink in March, and here we are, two Thanksgivings later, washing our dinner plates in the downstairs half-bath. I explain it to him so clearly, stringing all the events together exactly as they happened. \n\nAnd he follows me perfectly fine. He never tells me I am not making sense or that he cannot understand my point. He understands exactly what I am saying. He just... he just chooses to dismiss it, which is an entirely different thing from being hard to follow. He just shakes his head and goes back to his little manila folder. And the contractor, too, when I marched right up to him last Tuesday and asked him why his crew spent three hours sitting in their truck instead of finishing the drywall in the pantry, I was perfectly articulate. He understood every single word. He just gave me some nonsense excuse about waiting for the joint compound to dry. \n\nSo, no, absolutely not. My thoughts are perfectly organized. It is just that when you are the only one pointing out an ugly truth, people would rather pretend you are... well, I do not know, just being difficult, rather than admit that what you are saying makes perfect sense. It is incredibly isolating, doctor, it really is, but it has absolutely nothing to do with how I speak or put my thoughts together.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other unusual beliefs or suspicious ideas besides the remodeling and neighbors?\n\nPatient: Other unusual beliefs? Suspicious ideas? Oh, no. No, no, no. There is nothing else, doctor. I mean, why would there be? I am not a suspicious person by nature, I really am not. I have lived in this same town, in that exact same house, for thirty-five years. I was on the PTA when my son was little, I helped organize the neighborhood block party every single Fourth of July for over a decade... I have always trusted people. I have always believed that if you treat people fairly, they will treat you fairly in return. \n\nI don't go around thinking the mailman is reading my letters or that the cashier at the grocery store is shortchanging me on purpose. I don't think anyone is following me when I drive to the pharmacy. My life, outside of this absolute nightmare of a house remodel, is completely ordinary. I go to my book club on the second Thursday of every month—we're reading a very nice biography of Eleanor Roosevelt right now, though to be honest, I haven't been able to concentrate on it much lately because the dust from the hallway just gets everywhere and makes my eyes water. But my point is, my life is normal. \n\nI'm not sitting around making up grand conspiracies about the government or the banks or anything ridiculous like that. I don't have any strange ideas about the world. It is literally just this one, specific, undeniable situation with the contractor and Mrs. Gable and this endless, torturous construction project. If they would just finish the kitchen—if they would just put the sink in and patch the drywall and pack up their trucks and leave—I wouldn't have a single complaint in the world. I would go right back to baking my famous lemon pound cake for the church bake sale and tending to my garden. So, no, absolutely no other suspicious ideas. Just the very real, very exhausting reality of what they are doing to my home.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that other people can hear your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Hear my thoughts? Goodness, no. No, no. That sounds like something out of one of those science fiction movies my grandson used to watch on the television in the den—back when we actually had a den, before it was gutted down to the studs and left to rot, of course. \n\nNo, my thoughts are entirely my own, doctor. They are completely, one hundred percent private. I mean, if people could hear what I was thinking, well, I imagine that contractor would have packed up his tools and run off crying months ago. The things I think about that man and his so-called work ethic... well, they aren't very polite, let me tell you. I have some very strong opinions about how he runs his business, but they stay right here in my own head until I decide to share them. \n\nNo one can hear what's going on in my mind. I have to actually open my mouth and say it out loud for anyone to know what I'm thinking. And believe me, I do! I don't need anyone reading my mind because I am perfectly capable of speaking up for myself. I tell my husband exactly what I think, I tell the crew, I tell anyone who will listen. I have to use my words. It's just that... well, it's just that even when I do speak my mind, loud and clear, they all pretend not to hear me anyway. So why on earth would they need to read my thoughts? They don't even listen to my actual, physical voice when I'm standing right there in the middle of the kitchen—or what's left of the kitchen, anyway—pointing out that the new trim doesn't match the cabinets. \n\nSo, no, absolutely not. My thoughts are perfectly safe and private. No one is hearing them but me. If they want to know what I'm thinking, they have to listen to me speak, which is exactly what I've been trying to get my husband to do for two years now.\n\nDoctor: Have these concerns about the contractor and your neighbors been going on for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Six months? Oh, doctor, if it had only been six months, I might still have a shred of my sanity left. Six months would be a dream compared to this. No, it has been much, much longer than that. It has been going on for about a year and nine months now. A year and nine months! \n\nI know the exact timeline, you see, because it all started when they first came in to tear up the kitchen and the garden. It was supposed to be a simple update—new countertops, a fresh coat of paint, fixing up the patio area so I could enjoy my hydrangeas. But instead, it turned into this... this endless nightmare. They started the demolition, and almost immediately, the disruptions began. The noise, the dust, the constant coming and going of these strangers in my home, leaving their muddy boots on my hardwood floors... and then, of course, the contractor's behavior, and Mrs. Gable next door suddenly taking such an intense interest in everything they were doing. \n\nIt hasn't just been six months. It's been nearly two years of my life completely hijacked by this remodeling project that never seems to end. Two Thanksgivings, doctor, two! With no working oven and a tarp over where the bay window used to be. Every single day for a year and nine months, I have had to wake up and deal with the contractor's lies, the mess, and the absolute certainty that they are dragging this out on purpose. So, no, not six months. It has been a year and nine months of absolute turmoil since the very first day they started tearing my house apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these beliefs are affecting how you function in your daily life, like at home, with work, or with other responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Affecting my daily life? Well... I mean, how could it not? When you are living in a situation where you can't even trust the people standing right in your own kitchen, or the woman living twenty feet away from your front door... it changes things. It changes how you have to live. \n\nI used to be the sort of person who would chat with the workmen, offer them a glass of iced tea or a slice of that lemon pound cake I mentioned, you know? I liked having a friendly home. But I’ve completely stopped trusting them. I've stopped trusting certain neighbors, too—especially Mrs. Gable. I used to wave to her every single morning when I went out to get the newspaper, but now? Now I find myself waiting behind the living room curtains until I see her go back inside her house. I just can't be out there if she's out there, pretending to water her petunias while she's actually keeping tabs on my house. \n\nAnd as for the remodeling itself... well, it got to the point where my husband has had to step in and take over almost all of the decisions. He really has. I was supposed to be the one handling this project—I picked out the original fixtures, I drew up the plans for the patio—but I just couldn't deal with them directly anymore. Every time that contractor would show me a tile sample or ask me about the cabinet hardware, I just knew they were trying to distract me. I kept questioning them, asking them what they were really doing behind the drywall, why they were taking so long with the plumbing, what they were whispering about when they thought I was in the other room. \n\nIt was exhausting, doctor. Just utterly exhausting. I was spending my entire day just watching them like a hawk, questioning every single move they made, trying to figure out what they were actually up to. So my husband finally said he would handle the crew from now on. He talks to the contractor, he signs the invoices. But honestly, even with him taking over, I still have to warn him. I tell him, 'You can't just blindly trust what they tell you, you don't know what they're actually doing when your back is turned.' So, yes, it affects my life. It has completely taken over my daily routine, because I have to be constantly on guard in my own home.\n\nDoctor: If your husband or the contractor showed you clear proof that the remodel delays were due to ordinary problems, would you be able to change your mind about what is happening?\n\nPatient: Proof? Oh, doctor, what kind of 'proof' could they possibly have that I haven't already been subjected to? My husband—bless him, he really does try to keep the peace—he brings me these folders, these thick manila folders just stuffed with papers. Receipts from the home improvement stores, invoices with all these complicated itemized lists, shipping notices... He sits me down at the dining room table, since we don't have a kitchen table anymore, and he points at all these numbers and dates. He says, 'Look, the supply chain is backed up, the lumber was delayed, the city inspector was out sick.' \n\nExcuses. Just endless, endless excuses. I mean, I’m here talking to you right now because for nearly two years now, I’ve been absolutely sure—beyond a shadow of a doubt—that the contractor and some of the people on my block are quietly working together to cheat us. They are in it together, and they are doing it on purpose. They are deliberately keeping my kitchen half-wrong just to drag this out and drain us dry. \n\nAnd I mean half-wrong in the most literal sense! They will put up a beautiful new cabinet, but the door will be hung just a fraction of an inch crooked. Or they’ll install the new sink, but the hot and cold water handles are reversed. It is always, always half-wrong. And then here comes my husband with another piece of paper, or here comes the contractor with another long, drawn-out story about backordered hinges. \n\nBut no matter how many receipts my husband shows me, or how many excuses they give, it doesn't change what is actually happening right under my nose. Anyone can print a receipt, doctor. Anyone can type up a fake email from a plumbing supplier. But you can't fake the way Mrs. Gable looks at the foreman when he parks his truck, or the way they whisper at the end of my driveway when they think I'm not looking. So, no. No, I wouldn't change my mind, because a piece of paper doesn't erase what they've been doing to us for almost two years. I know what I see.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed that someone was controlling your thoughts or actions from outside your own mind?\n\nPatient: Controlling my thoughts? Heavens, no. No, doctor, that sounds like something out of a late-night science fiction movie. Well... I mean, if they *could* control my thoughts, if they had that kind of power, I'm quite sure they would have done it by now, wouldn't they? It would certainly make their little scheme a whole lot easier for them. They could just flip a switch in my brain and make me perfectly happy with crooked cabinet doors and backwards plumbing! \n\nBut no, absolutely not. My thoughts are entirely my own. In fact, my mind being so clear is exactly the problem for them. I am the only one around here who is actually thinking critically about all of this. My husband—bless him, like I said—his thoughts might as well be controlled by the contractor with the way he just blindly accepts every invoice and excuse they hand him. He just nods along. But me? I see exactly what is happening. \n\nNobody is pulling my strings or putting ideas into my head from the outside. The thoughts in my head are there simply because I have eyes and ears, and I actually pay attention to what goes on in my own home. Every action I take—like waiting behind the living room curtains until Mrs. Gable goes back inside—that is my own choice. I do that to protect myself, because I've thought it through and decided it's the safest way to get the morning paper. So no, no one is controlling my mind. I am in complete control of myself, which is probably why they find me so incredibly frustrating to deal with!\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your thoughts are being put into your mind by someone else?\n\nPatient: Put into my mind? Like... like someone is just slipping thoughts into my head while I'm not looking? No, doctor, no. I just told you, my thoughts are my own. I don't know where you're getting these ideas, but no one is putting anything into my mind. \n\nI mean, really, think about it. If someone could just put thoughts into my head, don't you think they would have put in the thought that I should just sit down, be quiet, and write another check to the contractor? That would be awfully convenient for them, wouldn't it? They could just beam a thought into my head that says, 'Oh, it's perfectly fine that the kitchen island is two inches off-center, just ignore it.' \n\nBut that hasn't happened. The thoughts I have are there because I put them there, based on what I see with my own two eyes. When I think about Mrs. Gable standing out there with her watering can, pretending to care about those petunias when she's really watching the crew unload drywall... that thought is mine. I formed it because I watched her do it, three days in a row, at exactly 8:15 in the morning. Nobody had to put that thought in my mind. I deduced it myself, because I am paying attention. I am the only one paying attention. So, no, absolutely not. My mind is my own, and the thoughts in it are mine, from start to finish.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods lasting at least a week when you felt unusually energized or euphoric, needed much less sleep, and your mind was racing?\n\nPatient: Unusually energized? Euphoric? Oh, heavens, no. I mean, how could anyone feel euphoric living in a construction zone for two solid years? It’s exhausting, doctor, utterly exhausting. I don't have periods of feeling energized; I have periods of feeling absolutely worn down to the bone because I am the only one keeping a watchful eye on everything. \n\nAnd as for needing less sleep... well, I certainly *get* less sleep, but it’s not because I don’t need it. I need my rest, I really do. I'm sixty-seven years old, I shouldn't be staying up half the night. But how can I sleep soundly when I know what’s going on? I lie there in bed, and my husband is snoring away—bless him, he can sleep through a hurricane—and I’m just listening. I’m listening for the sound of a truck pulling up, or footsteps on the gravel, or whispering outside the window. I have to stay alert because if I don't, who will? \n\nMy mind doesn't \"race\" like I'm on some kind of thrill ride. It just works. It processes what I've seen during the day. Like yesterday, when the foreman left a whole stack of two-by-fours right on the edge of the property line, practically touching Mrs. Gable's prized rose bushes. Now, anyone else might think he was just careless. But I lay awake thinking about it, turning it over in my head, because why would he put them exactly there? It’s a signal, obviously. It’s a way for them to communicate without speaking. So yes, I think about these things, I analyze them, because I have to. But it's not some manic, euphoric energy. It's just diligence. It’s just me trying to protect my home while everyone else is perfectly happy to let these people take advantage of us. So no, no weeks of feeling like that. Just endless weeks of being tired and having to stay sharp anyway.\n\nDoctor: What would it take for you to decide that the contractor and your neighbors are not intentionally targeting you?\n\nPatient: What would it take? Oh, doctor, that's... well, that's just the whole point of why I'm sitting in this chair right now, isn't it? I'm here because for nearly two years—two entire years of my life, starting right after Thanksgiving the year before last—I’ve been absolutely sure that the contractor, and some of the people on my block like Mrs. Gable and that young couple across the street, have been quietly working together. I know they are doing it on purpose. If they weren't, it would be finished by now, wouldn't it? Anyone could finish a kitchen in two years if they actually wanted to. So, to answer your question... I don't know if anything could convince me otherwise, because I'm not making this up. I'm just looking at the facts of what they are doing to me, day in and day out.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Oh, doctor, if it had only been six months, I might still have a shred of my sanity left. Six months would be a dream compared to this. No, it has been much, much longer than that. It has been going on for about a year and nine months now. A year and nine months! \n\nI know the exact timeline, you see, because it all started when they first came in to tear up the kitchen and the garden. It was supposed to be a simple update—new countertops, a fresh coat of paint, fixing up the patio area so I could enjoy my hydrangeas. But instead, it turned into this... this endless nightmare. They started the demolition, and almost immediately, the disruptions began. The noise, the dust, the constant coming and going of these strangers in my home, leaving their muddy boots on my hardwood floors... and then, of course, the contractor's behavior, and Mrs. Gable next door suddenly taking such an intense interest in everything they were doing. \n\nIt hasn't just been six months. It's been nearly two years of my life completely hijacked by this remodeling project that never seems to end. Two Thanksgivings, doctor, two! With no working oven and a tarp over where the bay window used to be. Every single day for a year and nine months, I have had to wake up and deal with the contractor's lies, the mess, and the absolute certainty that they are dragging this out on purpose. So, no, not six months. It has been a year and nine months of absolute turmoil since the very first day they started tearing my house apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these beliefs are affecting how you function in your daily life, like at home, with work, or with other responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Affecting my daily life? Well... I mean, how could it not? When you are living in a situation where you can't even trust the people standing right in your own kitchen, or the woman living twenty feet away from your front door... it changes things. It changes how you have to live. \n\nI used to be the sort of person who would chat with the workmen, offer them a glass of iced tea or a slice of that lemon pound cake I mentioned, you know? I liked having a friendly home. But I’ve completely stopped trusting them. I've stopped trusting certain neighbors, too—especially Mrs. Gable. I used to wave to her every single morning when I went out to get the newspaper, but now? Now I find myself waiting behind the living room curtains until I see her go back inside her house. I just can't be out there if she's out there, pretending to water her petunias while she's actually keeping tabs on my house. \n\nAnd as for the remodeling itself... well, it got to the point where my husband has had to step in and take over almost all of the decisions. He really has. I was supposed to be the one handling this project—I picked out the original fixtures, I drew up the plans for the patio—but I just couldn't deal with them directly anymore. Every time that contractor would show me a tile sample or ask me about the cabinet hardware, I just knew they were trying to distract me. I kept questioning them, asking them what they were really doing behind the drywall, why they were taking so long with the plumbing, what they were whispering about when they thought I was in the other room. \n\nIt was exhausting, doctor. Just utterly exhausting. I was spending my entire day just watching them like a hawk, questioning every single move they made, trying to figure out what they were actually up to. So my husband finally said he would handle the crew from now on. He talks to the contractor, he signs the invoices. But honestly, even with him taking over, I still have to warn him. I tell him, 'You can't just blindly trust what they tell you, you don't know what they're actually doing when your back is turned.' So, yes, it affects my life. It has completely taken over my daily routine, because I have to be constantly on guard in my own home.\n\nDoctor: If your husband or the contractor showed you clear proof that the remodel delays were due to ordinary problems, would you be able to change your mind about what is happening?\n\nPatient: Proof? Oh, doctor, what kind of 'proof' could they possibly have that I haven't already been subjected to? My husband—bless him, he really does try to keep the peace—he brings me these folders, these thick manila folders just stuffed with papers. Receipts from the home improvement stores, invoices with all these complicated itemized lists, shipping notices... He sits me down at the dining room table, since we don't have a kitchen table anymore, and he points at all these numbers and dates. He says, 'Look, the supply chain is backed up, the lumber was delayed, the city inspector was out sick.' \n\nExcuses. Just endless, endless excuses. I mean, I’m here talking to you right now because for nearly two years now, I’ve been absolutely sure—beyond a shadow of a doubt—that the contractor and some of the people on my block are quietly working together to cheat us. They are in it together, and they are doing it on purpose. They are deliberately keeping my kitchen half-wrong just to drag this out and drain us dry. \n\nAnd I mean half-wrong in the most literal sense! They will put up a beautiful new cabinet, but the door will be hung just a fraction of an inch crooked. Or they’ll install the new sink, but the hot and cold water handles are reversed. It is always, always half-wrong. And then here comes my husband with another piece of paper, or here comes the contractor with another long, drawn-out story about backordered hinges. \n\nBut no matter how many receipts my husband shows me, or how many excuses they give, it doesn't change what is actually happening right under my nose. Anyone can print a receipt, doctor. Anyone can type up a fake email from a plumbing supplier. But you can't fake the way Mrs. Gable looks at the foreman when he parks his truck, or the way they whisper at the end of my driveway when they think I'm not looking. So, no. No, I wouldn't change my mind, because a piece of paper doesn't erase what they've been doing to us for almost two years. I know what I see.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed that someone was controlling your thoughts or actions from outside your own mind?\n\nPatient: Controlling my thoughts? Heavens, no. No, doctor, that sounds like something out of a late-night science fiction movie. Well... I mean, if they *could* control my thoughts, if they had that kind of power, I'm quite sure they would have done it by now, wouldn't they? It would certainly make their little scheme a whole lot easier for them. They could just flip a switch in my brain and make me perfectly happy with crooked cabinet doors and backwards plumbing! \n\nBut no, absolutely not. My thoughts are entirely my own. In fact, my mind being so clear is exactly the problem for them. I am the only one around here who is actually thinking critically about all of this. My husband—bless him, like I said—his thoughts might as well be controlled by the contractor with the way he just blindly accepts every invoice and excuse they hand him. He just nods along. But me? I see exactly what is happening. \n\nNobody is pulling my strings or putting ideas into my head from the outside. The thoughts in my head are there simply because I have eyes and ears, and I actually pay attention to what goes on in my own home. Every action I take—like waiting behind the living room curtains until Mrs. Gable goes back inside—that is my own choice. I do that to protect myself, because I've thought it through and decided it's the safest way to get the morning paper. So no, no one is controlling my mind. I am in complete control of myself, which is probably why they find me so incredibly frustrating to deal with!\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your thoughts are being put into your mind by someone else?\n\nPatient: Put into my mind? Like... like someone is just slipping thoughts into my head while I'm not looking? No, doctor, no. I just told you, my thoughts are my own. I don't know where you're getting these ideas, but no one is putting anything into my mind. \n\nI mean, really, think about it. If someone could just put thoughts into my head, don't you think they would have put in the thought that I should just sit down, be quiet, and write another check to the contractor? That would be awfully convenient for them, wouldn't it? They could just beam a thought into my head that says, 'Oh, it's perfectly fine that the kitchen island is two inches off-center, just ignore it.' \n\nBut that hasn't happened. The thoughts I have are there because I put them there, based on what I see with my own two eyes. When I think about Mrs. Gable standing out there with her watering can, pretending to care about those petunias when she's really watching the crew unload drywall... that thought is mine. I formed it because I watched her do it, three days in a row, at exactly 8:15 in the morning. Nobody had to put that thought in my mind. I deduced it myself, because I am paying attention. I am the only one paying attention. So, no, absolutely not. My mind is my own, and the thoughts in it are mine, from start to finish.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods lasting at least a week when you felt unusually energized or euphoric, needed much less sleep, and your mind was racing?\n\nPatient: Unusually energized? Euphoric? Oh, heavens, no. I mean, how could anyone feel euphoric living in a construction zone for two solid years? It’s exhausting, doctor, utterly exhausting. I don't have periods of feeling energized; I have periods of feeling absolutely worn down to the bone because I am the only one keeping a watchful eye on everything. \n\nAnd as for needing less sleep... well, I certainly *get* less sleep, but it’s not because I don’t need it. I need my rest, I really do. I'm sixty-seven years old, I shouldn't be staying up half the night. But how can I sleep soundly when I know what’s going on? I lie there in bed, and my husband is snoring away—bless him, he can sleep through a hurricane—and I’m just listening. I’m listening for the sound of a truck pulling up, or footsteps on the gravel, or whispering outside the window. I have to stay alert because if I don't, who will? \n\nMy mind doesn't \"race\" like I'm on some kind of thrill ride. It just works. It processes what I've seen during the day. Like yesterday, when the foreman left a whole stack of two-by-fours right on the edge of the property line, practically touching Mrs. Gable's prized rose bushes. Now, anyone else might think he was just careless. But I lay awake thinking about it, turning it over in my head, because why would he put them exactly there? It’s a signal, obviously. It’s a way for them to communicate without speaking. So yes, I think about these things, I analyze them, because I have to. But it's not some manic, euphoric energy. It's just diligence. It’s just me trying to protect my home while everyone else is perfectly happy to let these people take advantage of us. So no, no weeks of feeling like that. Just endless weeks of being tired and having to stay sharp anyway.\n\nDoctor: What would it take for you to decide that the contractor and your neighbors are not intentionally targeting you?\n\nPatient: What would it take? Oh, doctor, that's... well, that's just the whole point of why I'm sitting in this chair right now, isn't it? I'm here because for nearly two years—two entire years of my life, starting right after Thanksgiving the year before last—I’ve been absolutely sure that the contractor, and some of the people on my block like Mrs. Gable and that young couple across the street, have been quietly working together. They are coordinating. They are working together to cheat us, to drag this out, and to keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. \n\nAnd you ask what it would take to change my mind? Well, what *could* change my mind when the evidence is right there in my house? My husband—bless him, he tries—he brings me these receipts, these long itemized lists from the contractor. He sits me down at the dining room table, the one that's covered in plastic sheeting, and he points to the numbers. He says, 'Look, Martha, they charged us for the extra copper piping, they charged us for the specialized hinges.' And I look at him, and I look at the receipts, and I tell him, 'A piece of paper doesn't change the fact that the cabinet doors still don't close right, and the sink is still draining slowly.' \n\nNo matter how many receipts he shows me, or how many excuses the foreman gives me about supply chain issues or backordered materials, I know what I see. I know they are doing it on purpose. If they weren't, it would be finished by now, wouldn't it? Anyone could finish a kitchen in two years if they actually wanted to. So, to answer your question... I don't know if anything could convince me otherwise, because I'm not making this up. I'm just looking at the facts of what they are doing to me, day in and day out.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hearing voices? Like... like imaginary people talking to me? Oh, absolutely not. No, doctor, my hearing is perfectly fine. Well, I do have a little bit of ringing in my left ear sometimes when the house gets too quiet, but that's just from getting older, not voices. I certainly don't hear anything that isn't really there. 

What I *do* hear are the very real things happening right outside my own windows. I hear the foreman—Craig, I think his name is, though he mumbles so much it's hard to tell—I hear him out there on his cell phone, keeping his voice low so I can't catch every single word. But I hear him. And I hear Mrs. Gable's back door click open, just a tiny little *snick* of the latch, right after Craig hangs up. That’s not a hallucination, doctor. That’s just me having good ears and actually paying attention to my surroundings. 

And as for seeing things? Heavens, no. I don't see visions or ghosts or whatever it is you're implying. I wear bifocals, sure, but my prescription was just updated in March, and I see exactly what is in front of me. I see the dirty handprints they leave on the new window panes on purpose. I see the way the tile in the half-bath doesn't line up with the grout lines in the hallway. 

Now, my husband, he’ll walk right past that tile and tell me it looks fine. He doesn't see it. But does that mean I'm hallucinating the crooked tile? Of course not! It just means he isn't looking closely enough. He just wants this whole nightmare to be over, so he ignores the details. He’s always been like that, bless him, just smoothing things over. But I can't do that. I have to see it, I have to document it, because if I don't point it out, they get away with it. They want me to think I'm seeing things, I'm sure of it, but my eyes work just fine. So, no, no imaginary voices or visions. Just the reality of what they are doing to us, plain as day, right there in my own home.

They have an excuse for every single thing! But no matter how many receipts he waves in my face or how many lies they spin to him, I know they are doing this to us on purpose. It’s deliberate, and it's just exhausting having to be the only one who actually sees what they're up to.\n\nDoctor: What is the most convincing evidence, in your mind, that the contractor and neighbors are intentionally targeting you?\n\nPatient: Evidence? Well... I mean, if you're asking for a single, undeniable piece of proof, like... like a secret recording or a document where they've written down their whole plan, I... I don't really have anything like that. I'm not sure there is one specific thing I could just hand over to you and say, 'Here, look at this, this proves it all.' It's not like they're leaving a paper trail for me to find. \n\nMy husband is always asking for the exact same thing, you know. He sits there at the kitchen table—well, the folding card table we've had to use for twenty-four months now because the center island isn't finished—and he says, 'Show me the proof, show me the evidence.' And I try, I really try to explain it to him, but it's so hard to make someone understand when it's... well, when it's a hundred little things happening every single day. It's the way Mrs. Gable from next door suddenly decides to water her dead azaleas right when the contractor's truck pulls up. Or the way the workers all stop talking the second I walk into my own hallway. \n\nBut if you're asking me to pick out the absolute most convincing evidence... I just don't know. I really don't. I don't have a smoking gun for you. I just know what I've been living through, day in and day out, and I know what I see with my own two eyes, even if I can't put it into a neat little manila folder for you and my husband.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing or seeing things that other people don’t seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Hearing things? Like... like voices whispering to me, or something? Oh, heavens no. No, no, no. I know exactly what you're getting at with a question like that, doctor. I really do. My husband probably put you up to asking that, didn't he? He thinks just because I'm the only one willing to look at what's actually going on with this remodel, that I must be losing my mind. But no, I don't hear voices or see things that aren't there. My hearing is perfectly fine. I hear the contractor's heavy boots stomping on my hardwood floors, I hear their tools, I hear Mrs. Gable's sprinkler system ticking on next door. Just regular, everyday sounds that anyone would hear. \n\nAnd as for seeing things... well, I see exactly what is right in front of my face, plain as day. I see the unfinished drywall. I see those exposed wires hanging down like spiderwebs over the space where my new kitchen sink was supposed to be installed so long ago. I see the dust settling on absolutely everything I own, ruining my good furniture. But I'm not seeing visions, if that's what you mean. I'm not making things up in my head. I'm just a woman who wants her home back in order, and it's just so incredibly frustrating because the minute you try to explain what's really happening—what these people are actually doing to us—everyone immediately jumps to, 'Oh, she must be imagining things, she must be seeing things.' \n\nBut I'm not. I'm just observing the reality of my ruined house, day after day, week after week, while my husband just pretends it's nothing but normal delays. It's not normal to live in a torn-up construction zone for two whole years, doctor, it really just isn't, and you certainly don't need to be seeing things that aren't there to know that.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your thoughts or speech are hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Hard to follow? Me? Oh, certainly not. No one has ever said anything like that to me. I have always prided myself on being very clear, very... well, very direct, really. I mean, you can hear me right now, can't you? I speak as plainly as anyone. If anything, I think I am too clear for some people's liking, especially when it comes to this whole house situation. \n\nWhen I sit down with my husband at that awful folding table, I do not just babble. I lay it out for him, step by step, completely logically. I tell him to look at the timeline. I point out the fact that they took out the old sink in March, and here we are, two Thanksgivings later, washing our dinner plates in the downstairs half-bath. I explain it to him so clearly, stringing all the events together exactly as they happened. \n\nAnd he follows me perfectly fine. He never tells me I am not making sense or that he cannot understand my point. He understands exactly what I am saying. He just... he just chooses to dismiss it, which is an entirely different thing from being hard to follow. He just shakes his head and goes back to his little manila folder. And the contractor, too, when I marched right up to him last Tuesday and asked him why his crew spent three hours sitting in their truck instead of finishing the drywall in the pantry, I was perfectly articulate. He understood every single word. He just gave me some nonsense excuse about waiting for the joint compound to dry. \n\nSo, no, absolutely not. My thoughts are perfectly organized. It is just that when you are the only one pointing out an ugly truth, people would rather pretend you are... well, I do not know, just being difficult, rather than admit that what you are saying makes perfect sense. It is incredibly isolating, doctor, it really is, but it has absolutely nothing to do with how I speak or put my thoughts together.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other unusual beliefs or suspicious ideas besides the remodeling and neighbors?\n\nPatient: Other unusual beliefs? Suspicious ideas? Oh, no. No, no, no. There is nothing else, doctor. I mean, why would there be? I am not a suspicious person by nature, I really am not. I have lived in this same town, in that exact same house, for thirty-five years. I was on the PTA when my son was little, I helped organize the neighborhood block party every single Fourth of July for over a decade... I have always trusted people. I have always believed that if you treat people fairly, they will treat you fairly in return. \n\nI don't go around thinking the mailman is reading my letters or that the cashier at the grocery store is shortchanging me on purpose. I don't think anyone is following me when I drive to the pharmacy. My life, outside of this absolute nightmare of a house remodel, is completely ordinary. I go to my book club on the second Thursday of every month—we're reading a very nice biography of Eleanor Roosevelt right now, though to be honest, I haven't been able to concentrate on it much lately because the dust from the hallway just gets everywhere and makes my eyes water. But my point is, my life is normal. \n\nI'm not sitting around making up grand conspiracies about the government or the banks or anything ridiculous like that. I don't have any strange ideas about the world. It is literally just this one, specific, undeniable situation with the contractor and Mrs. Gable and this endless, torturous construction project. If they would just finish the kitchen—if they would just put the sink in and patch the drywall and pack up their trucks and leave—I wouldn't have a single complaint in the world. I would go right back to baking my famous lemon pound cake for the church bake sale and tending to my garden. So, no, absolutely no other suspicious ideas. Just the very real, very exhausting reality of what they are doing to my home.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that other people can hear your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Hear my thoughts? Goodness, no. No, no. That sounds like something out of one of those science fiction movies my grandson used to watch on the television in the den—back when we actually had a den, before it was gutted down to the studs and left to rot, of course. \n\nNo, my thoughts are entirely my own, doctor. They are completely, one hundred percent private. I mean, if people could hear what I was thinking, well, I imagine that contractor would have packed up his tools and run off crying months ago. The things I think about that man and his so-called work ethic... well, they aren't very polite, let me tell you. I have some very strong opinions about how he runs his business, but they stay right here in my own head until I decide to share them. \n\nNo one can hear what's going on in my mind. I have to actually open my mouth and say it out loud for anyone to know what I'm thinking. And believe me, I do! I don't need anyone reading my mind because I am perfectly capable of speaking up for myself. I tell my husband exactly what I think, I tell the crew, I tell anyone who will listen. I have to use my words. It's just that... well, it's just that even when I do speak my mind, loud and clear, they all pretend not to hear me anyway. So why on earth would they need to read my thoughts? They don't even listen to my actual, physical voice when I'm standing right there in the middle of the kitchen—or what's left of the kitchen, anyway—pointing out that the new trim doesn't match the cabinets. \n\nSo, no, absolutely not. My thoughts are perfectly safe and private. No one is hearing them but me. If they want to know what I'm thinking, they have to listen to me speak, which is exactly what I've been trying to get my husband to do for two years now.\n\nDoctor: Have these concerns about the contractor and your neighbors been going on for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Six months? Oh, doctor, if it had only been six months, I might still have a shred of my sanity left. Six months would be a dream compared to this. No, it has been much, much longer than that. It has been going on for about a year and nine months now. A year and nine months! \n\nI know the exact timeline, you see, because it all started when they first came in to tear up the kitchen and the garden. It was supposed to be a simple update—new countertops, a fresh coat of paint, fixing up the patio area so I could enjoy my hydrangeas. But instead, it turned into this... this endless nightmare. They started the demolition, and almost immediately, the disruptions began. The noise, the dust, the constant coming and going of these strangers in my home, leaving their muddy boots on my hardwood floors... and then, of course, the contractor's behavior, and Mrs. Gable next door suddenly taking such an intense interest in everything they were doing. \n\nIt hasn't just been six months. It's been nearly two years of my life completely hijacked by this remodeling project that never seems to end. Two Thanksgivings, doctor, two! With no working oven and a tarp over where the bay window used to be. Every single day for a year and nine months, I have had to wake up and deal with the contractor's lies, the mess, and the absolute certainty that they are dragging this out on purpose. So, no, not six months. It has been a year and nine months of absolute turmoil since the very first day they started tearing my house apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these beliefs are affecting how you function in your daily life, like at home, with work, or with other responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Affecting my daily life? Well... I mean, how could it not? When you are living in a situation where you can't even trust the people standing right in your own kitchen, or the woman living twenty feet away from your front door... it changes things. It changes how you have to live. \n\nI used to be the sort of person who would chat with the workmen, offer them a glass of iced tea or a slice of that lemon pound cake I mentioned, you know? I liked having a friendly home. But I’ve completely stopped trusting them. I've stopped trusting certain neighbors, too—especially Mrs. Gable. I used to wave to her every single morning when I went out to get the newspaper, but now? Now I find myself waiting behind the living room curtains until I see her go back inside her house. I just can't be out there if she's out there, pretending to water her petunias while she's actually keeping tabs on my house. \n\nAnd as for the remodeling itself... well, it got to the point where my husband has had to step in and take over almost all of the decisions. He really has. I was supposed to be the one handling this project—I picked out the original fixtures, I drew up the plans for the patio—but I just couldn't deal with them directly anymore. Every time that contractor would show me a tile sample or ask me about the cabinet hardware, I just knew they were trying to distract me. I kept questioning them, asking them what they were really doing behind the drywall, why they were taking so long with the plumbing, what they were whispering about when they thought I was in the other room. \n\nIt was exhausting, doctor. Just utterly exhausting. I was spending my entire day just watching them like a hawk, questioning every single move they made, trying to figure out what they were actually up to. So my husband finally said he would handle the crew from now on. He talks to the contractor, he signs the invoices. But honestly, even with him taking over, I still have to warn him. I tell him, 'You can't just blindly trust what they tell you, you don't know what they're actually doing when your back is turned.' So, yes, it affects my life. It has completely taken over my daily routine, because I have to be constantly on guard in my own home.\n\nDoctor: If your husband or the contractor showed you clear proof that the remodel delays were due to ordinary problems, would you be able to change your mind about what is happening?\n\nPatient: Proof? Oh, doctor, what kind of 'proof' could they possibly have that I haven't already been subjected to? My husband—bless him, he really does try to keep the peace—he brings me these folders, these thick manila folders just stuffed with papers. Receipts from the home improvement stores, invoices with all these complicated itemized lists, shipping notices... He sits me down at the dining room table, since we don't have a kitchen table anymore, and he points at all these numbers and dates. He says, 'Look, the supply chain is backed up, the lumber was delayed, the city inspector was out sick.' \n\nExcuses. Just endless, endless excuses. I mean, I’m here talking to you right now because for nearly two years now, I’ve been absolutely sure—beyond a shadow of a doubt—that the contractor and some of the people on my block are quietly working together to cheat us. They are in it together, and they are doing it on purpose. They are deliberately keeping my kitchen half-wrong just to drag this out and drain us dry. \n\nAnd I mean half-wrong in the most literal sense! They will put up a beautiful new cabinet, but the door will be hung just a fraction of an inch crooked. Or they’ll install the new sink, but the hot and cold water handles are reversed. It is always, always half-wrong. And then here comes my husband with another piece of paper, or here comes the contractor with another long, drawn-out story about backordered hinges. \n\nBut no matter how many receipts my husband shows me, or how many excuses they give, it doesn't change what is actually happening right under my nose. Anyone can print a receipt, doctor. Anyone can type up a fake email from a plumbing supplier. But you can't fake the way Mrs. Gable looks at the foreman when he parks his truck, or the way they whisper at the end of my driveway when they think I'm not looking. So, no. No, I wouldn't change my mind, because a piece of paper doesn't erase what they've been doing to us for almost two years. I know what I see.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed that someone was controlling your thoughts or actions from outside your own mind?\n\nPatient: Controlling my thoughts? Heavens, no. No, doctor, that sounds like something out of a late-night science fiction movie. Well... I mean, if they *could* control my thoughts, if they had that kind of power, I'm quite sure they would have done it by now, wouldn't they? It would certainly make their little scheme a whole lot easier for them. They could just flip a switch in my brain and make me perfectly happy with crooked cabinet doors and backwards plumbing! \n\nBut no, absolutely not. My thoughts are entirely my own. In fact, my mind being so clear is exactly the problem for them. I am the only one around here who is actually thinking critically about all of this. My husband—bless him, like I said—his thoughts might as well be controlled by the contractor with the way he just blindly accepts every invoice and excuse they hand him. He just nods along. But me? I see exactly what is happening. \n\nNobody is pulling my strings or putting ideas into my head from the outside. The thoughts in my head are there simply because I have eyes and ears, and I actually pay attention to what goes on in my own home. Every action I take—like waiting behind the living room curtains until Mrs. Gable goes back inside—that is my own choice. I do that to protect myself, because I've thought it through and decided it's the safest way to get the morning paper. So no, no one is controlling my mind. I am in complete control of myself, which is probably why they find me so incredibly frustrating to deal with!\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your thoughts are being put into your mind by someone else?\n\nPatient: Put into my mind? Like... like someone is just slipping thoughts into my head while I'm not looking? No, doctor, no. I just told you, my thoughts are my own. I don't know where you're getting these ideas, but no one is putting anything into my mind. \n\nI mean, really, think about it. If someone could just put thoughts into my head, don't you think they would have put in the thought that I should just sit down, be quiet, and write another check to the contractor? That would be awfully convenient for them, wouldn't it? They could just beam a thought into my head that says, 'Oh, it's perfectly fine that the kitchen island is two inches off-center, just ignore it.' \n\nBut that hasn't happened. The thoughts I have are there because I put them there, based on what I see with my own two eyes. When I think about Mrs. Gable standing out there with her watering can, pretending to care about those petunias when she's really watching the crew unload drywall... that thought is mine. I formed it because I watched her do it, three days in a row, at exactly 8:15 in the morning. Nobody had to put that thought in my mind. I deduced it myself, because I am paying attention. I am the only one paying attention. So, no, absolutely not. My mind is my own, and the thoughts in it are mine, from start to finish.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods lasting at least a week when you felt unusually energized or euphoric, needed much less sleep, and your mind was racing?\n\nPatient: Unusually energized? Euphoric? Oh, heavens, no. I mean, how could anyone feel euphoric living in a construction zone for two solid years? It’s exhausting, doctor, utterly exhausting. I don't have periods of feeling energized; I have periods of feeling absolutely worn down to the bone because I am the only one keeping a watchful eye on everything. \n\nAnd as for needing less sleep... well, I certainly *get* less sleep, but it’s not because I don’t need it. I need my rest, I really do. I'm sixty-seven years old, I shouldn't be staying up half the night. But how can I sleep soundly when I know what’s going on? I lie there in bed, and my husband is snoring away—bless him, he can sleep through a hurricane—and I’m just listening. I’m listening for the sound of a truck pulling up, or footsteps on the gravel, or whispering outside the window. I have to stay alert because if I don't, who will? \n\nMy mind doesn't \"race\" like I'm on some kind of thrill ride. It just works. It processes what I've seen during the day. Like yesterday, when the foreman left a whole stack of two-by-fours right on the edge of the property line, practically touching Mrs. Gable's prized rose bushes. Now, anyone else might think he was just careless. But I lay awake thinking about it, turning it over in my head, because why would he put them exactly there? It’s a signal, obviously. It’s a way for them to communicate without speaking. So yes, I think about these things, I analyze them, because I have to. But it's not some manic, euphoric energy. It's just diligence. It’s just me trying to protect my home while everyone else is perfectly happy to let these people take advantage of us. So no, no weeks of feeling like that. Just endless weeks of being tired and having to stay sharp anyway.\n\nDoctor: What would it take for you to decide that the contractor and your neighbors are not intentionally targeting you?\n\nPatient: What would it take? Oh, doctor, that's... well, that's just the whole point of why I'm sitting in this chair right now, isn't it? I'm here because for nearly two years—two entire years of my life, starting right after Thanksgiving the year before last—I’ve been absolutely sure that the contractor, and some of the people on my block like Mrs. Gable and that young couple across the street, have been quietly working together. They are coordinating. They are working together to cheat us, to drag this out, and to keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. \n\nAnd you ask what it would take to change my mind? Well, what *could* change my mind when the evidence is right there in my house? My husband—bless him, he tries—he brings me these receipts, these long itemized lists from the contractor. He sits me down at the dining room table, the one that's covered in plastic sheeting, and he points to the numbers. He says, 'Look, Martha, they charged us for the extra copper piping, they charged us for the specialized hinges.' And I look at him, and I look at the receipts, and I tell him, 'A piece of paper doesn't change the fact that the cabinet doors still don't close right, and the sink is still draining slowly.' \n\nNo matter how many receipts he shows me, or how many excuses the foreman gives me about supply chain issues or backordered materials, I know what I see. I know they are doing it on purpose. If they weren't, it would be finished by now, wouldn't it? Anyone could finish a kitchen in two years if they actually wanted to. So, to answer your question... I don't know if anything could convince me otherwise, because I'm not making this up. I'm just looking at the facts of what they are doing to me, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any hallucinations, like hearing voices or seeing things that others do not, at any point during this problem?\n\nPatient: Hearing voices? Like... like imaginary people talking to me? Oh, absolutely not. No, doctor, my hearing is perfectly fine. Well, I do have a little bit of ringing in my left ear sometimes when the house gets too quiet, but that's just from getting older, not voices. I certainly don't hear anything that isn't really there. \n\nWhat I *do* hear are the very real things happening right outside my own windows. I hear the foreman—Craig, I think his name is, though he mumbles so much it's hard to tell—I hear him out there on his cell phone, keeping his voice low so I can't catch every single word. But I hear him. And I hear Mrs. Gable's back door click open, just a tiny little *snick* of the latch, right after Craig hangs up. That’s not a hallucination, doctor. That’s just me having good ears and actually paying attention to my surroundings. \n\nAnd as for seeing things? Heavens, no. I don't see visions or ghosts or whatever it is you're implying. I wear bifocals, sure, but my prescription was just updated in March, and I see exactly what is in front of me. I see the dirty handprints they leave on the new window panes on purpose. I see the way the tile in the half-bath doesn't line up with the grout lines in the hallway. \n\nNow, my husband, he’ll walk right past that tile and tell me it looks fine. He doesn't see it. But does that mean I'm hallucinating the crooked tile? Of course not! It just means he isn't looking closely enough. He just wants this whole nightmare to be over, so he ignores the details. He’s always been like that, bless him, just smoothing things over. But I can't do that. I have to see it, I have to document it, because if I don't point it out, they get away with it. They want me to think I'm seeing things, I'm sure of it, but my eyes work just fine. So, no, no imaginary voices or visions. Just the reality of what they are doing to us, plain as day, right there in my own home."
